Minimax Optimal Estimation of Transport-Growth Pairs in Unbalanced Optimal Transport
Donlapark Ponnoprat 1 , Noboru Isobe 2 and Masaaki Imaizumi 2,3,4

Abstract
. Unbalanced optimal transport (UOT) extends classical optimal transport to measures with different total masses, but statistical guarantees for Monge-type estimation remain limited. We study unbalanced transport with quadratic cost and Kullback-Leibler marginal penalties and argue that the natural population target is not a map alone, but a transport-growth pair. Consequently, we develop two estimators for the transport-growth pairs under several setups: an optimal transport plan-based estimator for a general case, and a kernel-based estimator for a case with smooth densities. We also show that an error of the estimator achieves the minimax optimal rate by deriving a matching lower bound of the minimax risk. Our main technical contribution is a value-based stability reduction that converts perturbations of the UOT objective into transport and growth risks through a UOT gap condition. These results provide a statistical foundation for Monge-type estimation in unbalanced optimal transport.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

Optimal transport (OT) provides a principled geometric language for comparing probability distributions and has become a standard tool in statistics and machine learning. In statistical applications, however, the underlying population measures are rarely observed directly; instead, one must infer transport objects from finite samples. For balanced OT, this question is now supported by a substantial statistical theory. Smooth transport maps admit minimax analysis via semi-dual curvature and growth arguments
[
21
]
, while plug-in, barycentric-projection, and entropic estimators have been analyzed in discrete, semi-discrete, and smooth regimes
[
12
,
28
,
25
,
24
]
. More recently, the balanced theory has expanded to general function-space analyses and sharper stability reductions for plug-in estimators
[
13
,
3
]
.

Many datasets are not naturally balanced: the total masses may differ, unmatched observations may be present, and mass creation or destruction may be an intrinsic feature of the phenomenon under study. Unbalanced optimal transport (UOT) addresses this issue by relaxing the hard marginal constraints of OT and penalizing deviations of the plan marginals from the reference measures. This viewpoint underlies the modern entropy-transport framework and includes important models such as logarithmic entropy-transport and the Wasserstein-Fisher-Rao/Hellinger-Kantorovich geometry
[
9
,
22
,
32
,
18
]
. It has also generated a large computational and applied literature, including Sinkhorn-type solvers and scalable parameterizations
[
27
,
35
,
17
,
41
]
, with applications ranging from single-cell dynamics to growth modeling and shape analysis
[
33
,
31
,
11
,
2
]
.

Despite this rapid development, the statistical theory of UOT remains less developed, especially for Monge-type objects. For the UOT model studied below,
[
37
,
38
]
derived semi-dual formulations and established global Bregman-type stability for the corresponding objectives, yielding the first fast statistical rates for UOT semi-dual potentials. More recently, statistical properties of unbalanced Kantorovich-Rubinstein quantities have been analyzed for finitely supported measures and for spatio-temporal point-process models
[
20
,
29
]
. However, compared with the balanced case, a plug-in or near-minimax theory for Monge-type UOT maps is still largely missing.

A key difficulty is structural. In balanced OT, the specific form of the transport objective allows us to bound the transport error by exploiting the semi-dual form of the transport problem
[
21
,
24
,
3
]
. UOT, by contrast, has a different objective where the hard marginal constraints are replaced with divergence penalties; this modification introduces a
growth map
that specified how the masses will contract or expand after the transport.
Consequently, a rigorous statistical theory for UOT must account for the errors of transport map and the growth map estimation.

1.2.
Our contribution

In this paper, we develop a statistical theory for Monge-type estimation in unbalanced optimal transport with KL marginal penalties. In particular, we develop two estimators for the transport-growth pair, then study their estimation error.

Concretely, our contributions are as follows.

•

Estimators for the transport-growth pair:
We formulate the Monge-type statistical target for UOT as the transport-growth pair, then develop two estimators: a plan-based estimator obtained from a discrete UOT plan, and a smooth plug-in estimator based on regularized marginal estimates.

•

Prove optimality:
We prove that the estimators achieve the minimax optimal convergence rate, by deriving both an upper bound of their estimation error and a corresponding lower bound of the minimax risk. This result improves upon the existing evaluation, as summarized in Table
1.2
.

•

Proof technique by stability bound:
To analyze this target, we establish a stability bound and a first-order expansion of the UOT objective under perturbations of the marginals, which isolates the UOT-specific analytic step from the choice of marginal estimator.
By employing a stability-based proof, we circumvent the limitations of proofs using semi-dual potentials and enable the evaluation of optimality.

1.3.
Notation

Throughout,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the ambient domain. We write
C(\Omega)
for the space of continuous real-valued functions on
\Omega
,
C_{b}(\Omega)
for the bounded continuous functions, and
\mathrm{Diff}(\Omega)
for the class of diffeomorphisms from
\Omega
onto itself whenever differentiability is imposed. The set of finite positive Radon measures on
\Omega
is denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)
, and
\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
denotes the Borel probability measures on
\Omega
. For a measurable map
T:\Omega\to\Omega
and a measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)
,
T_{\#}\mu
denotes the pushforward of
\mu
. For
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega\times\Omega)
, we write
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
for its first and second marginals, respectively. If
\eta,\sigma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)
have the same total mass, then
\Pi(\eta,\sigma)
denotes the set of couplings between them. We use
\|\cdot\|
for the Euclidean norm,
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}
for the operator norm,
\mathbf{1}\{\cdot\}
for the indicator function, and
D_{\mathrm{KL}}
for the KL divergence. The Legendre-Fenchel transform of a convex function
F
is denoted by
F^{*}
.
For real numbers
a<b
, the clipping function is
\clip_{[a,b]}(t)\coloneqq\min\{b,\max\{a,t\}\}
.

2.
Unbalanced Optimal Transport with Quadratic Cost

We briefly review the unbalanced optimal transport in the
Gaussian-Hellinger
case. For a complete treatment of the general case, see
[
22
,
32
,
18
]
.
Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded convex domain and
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)
are finite positive Radon measures. The
c-transform
of a function
\varphi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is defined as
\varphi^{c}(x)=\inf_{y}\{\|x-y\|^{2}/2-\varphi(y)\}
. We say that
\varphi
is
c-concave
if
\varphi_{0}=\psi^{c}
for some
\psi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
.

2.1.
Transport formulations

Monge problem

: We introduce a Monge-like form of the unbalanced optimal transport setup.
A
transport-growth pair
consists of a measurable map
T:\Omega\to\Omega
and a measurable weight
\lambda:\Omega\to[0,\infty)
, and acts on
\mu
through the weighted pushforward

\displaystyle(T,\lambda)_{\#}\mu\coloneqq T_{\#}(\lambda^{2}\mu).

(1)

The map
\lambda
is referred to as the
growth factor
, since it rescales mass after the transport.
In the Gaussian-Hellinger case, we have the following cone cost by

C((x,r),(y,s))^{2}\coloneqq r^{2}+s^{2}-2rse^{-\|x-y\|^{2}/4},\qquad x,y\in\Omega,\ r,s\geq 0.

Using this cone cost, we obtain the

unbalanced Monge problem
for the Gaussian-Hellinger case:

\displaystyle\mathrm{UM}(\mu,\nu)\coloneqq\inf_{(T,\lambda):(T,\lambda)_{\#}\mu=\nu}\int_{\Omega}\bigl(1+\lambda(x)^{2}-2\lambda(x)e^{-\|x-T(x)\|^{2}/4}\bigr)d\mu(x).

(2)

In contrast to balanced OT, where only a transport map
T_{0}
appears, UOT also requires a growth map
\lambda_{0}
to reconcile the discrepancy between the total masses of
\mu
and
\nu
.

Kantorovich problem

: We consider another problem for the unbalanced optimal transport by optimizing over plans
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega\times\Omega)
instead of deterministic transport-growth pairs for the Monge problem. Specifically, the Kantorovich problem associated to (
2
) is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)\coloneqq\inf_{\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega\times\Omega)}\left\{\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{2}d\gamma^{\prime}(x,y)+D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\mid\mu)+D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}\mid\nu)\right\}.

(3)

The corresponding dual problem reads:

\displaystyle\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\varphi,\psi)\in C_{b}(\Omega)^{2}\\
\varphi(x)+\psi(y)\leq\|x-y\|^{2}/2\end{subarray}}\left\{\int_{\Omega}\bigl(1-e^{-\varphi(x)}\bigr)d\mu(x)+\int_{\Omega}\bigl(1-e^{-\psi(y)}\bigr)d\nu(y)\right\}.

(4)

Let
(\varphi_{0},\psi_{0})
denote an optimal dual pair for (
4
), and we refer to them as
potentials
. Existence of the solutions of (
2
) and (
4
) are guaranteed by the following theorem:

Theorem 1

(
[
18
]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be bounded and convex, let
\mu=\rho_{0}dx
with
\rho_{0}>0
a.e., and let
\nu\ll dx
be supported in
\Omega
. Then there exists a
\mu
-a.e. unique
c
-concave
\varphi_{0}
such that
(\varphi_{0},\psi_{0})
solves the dual problem (
4
) with
\psi_{0}=\varphi^{c}_{0}
, and
\varphi_{0}
uniquely determines a solution
(T_{0},\lambda_{0})
of the Monge problem (
2
) via:

\displaystyle T_{0}(x)=x-\nabla\varphi_{0}(x),\qquad\lambda_{0}(x)=\exp\left(-\varphi_{0}(x)+\frac{1}{4}\|\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)\|^{2}\right).

(5)

Useful form:

Following Theorem
1
, we define
active source marginal

\gamma_{0}=e^{-\varphi_{0}}\mu
and
active-source factor

a_{0}(x)\coloneqq e^{-\varphi_{0}(x)/2}
.
It follows from the complementary slackness that
\varphi_{0}(x)+\psi_{0}(T_{0}(x))=\|x-T_{0}(x)\|^{2}/2
. We thus have the following gradient-free formulation of
T_{0}
and
\lambda_{0}
:

\displaystyle T_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{y\in\Omega}\left\{\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{2}-\psi_{0}(y)\right\},\qquad\lambda_{0}(x)=a_{0}(x)^{2}\exp\left(\frac{1}{4}\|x-T_{0}(x)\|^{2}\right).

(6)

2.2.
Statistical estimation problem of the Monge map

Given finite positive measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)
, we consider total masses

M_{\mu}\coloneqq\mu(\Omega)
and
M_{\nu}\coloneqq\nu(\Omega)

and define the normalized probability measures
\bar{\mu}\coloneqq{\mu}/{M_{\mu}},\bar{\nu}\coloneqq{\nu}/{M_{\nu}}
.
Given sample sizes
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
, we observe mutually independent samples
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\sim\bar{\mu}
and
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}\sim\bar{\nu}
.
Because the data are sampled from the normalized laws
\bar{\mu}
and
\bar{\nu}
, the total masses
M_{\mu}
and
M_{\nu}
are not identifiable from the samples alone. We therefore assume either that these masses are known, or that additional estimators
\widehat{M}_{\mu}
and
\widehat{M}_{\nu}
are available from external information.
We then define atomic weights

\widehat{\mu}_{i}\coloneqq\widehat{\mu}_{n}(\{X_{i}\})=\widehat{M}_{\mu}/n,\widehat{\nu}_{j}\coloneqq\widehat{\nu}_{m}(\{Y_{j}\})=\widehat{M}_{\nu}/m
and the weighted empirical measures

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\widehat{M}_{\mu}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}},\qquad\widehat{\nu}_{m}\coloneqq\frac{\widehat{M}_{\nu}}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{Y_{j}}.

(7)

Our objective is to estimate the population transport-growth pair
(T_{0},\lambda_{0})
associated with
(\mu,\nu)
from the observed samples and the mass estimators. Our proofs also estimate the auxiliary active-source factor
a_{0}=e^{-\varphi_{0}/2}
, since it is the quantity directly encoded by the source marginal
\gamma_{0}=a_{0}^{2}\mu
of the Kantorovich plan.

Remark 1
.

The construction of
\widehat{M}_{\mu}
and
\widehat{M}_{\nu}
depends on the observation scheme.
If external total-mass measurements are available, they can be plugged in directly. In Poisson or more general point-process models, the observed counts naturally carry mass information; see Appendix
C.2
and the recent UOT analyses of
[
20
,
29
]
.

3.
Estimator design

We propose two estimators: (i) a plan-based estimator and (ii) a kernel plugin estimator; the former can handle high dimensional data, while the latter is adaptive to the smoothness of the densities.

We introduce some notations: for a nonnegative matrix
G=(G_{ij})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n\times m}
, define the discrete marginals
(G_{0})_{i}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{m}G_{ij}
and
(G_{1})_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{ij}
.
With
F(r)=r\log r-r+1
, the corresponding discrete KL penalty between mass vectors is

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(G_{0}\mid\widehat{\mu}_{n})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\mu}_{i}F\bigl((G_{0})_{i}/\widehat{\mu}_{i}\bigr).

3.1.
Plan-based estimator

This estimator is constructed by solving the Kantorovich problem under the empirical measures
\widehat{\mu}_{n}
and
\widehat{\nu}_{m}
and using its optimal transport plan obtained as a matrix, based on the following steps.

(i) Transport plan estimation:

Let the discrete cost matrix be
C_{ij}\coloneqq\|X_{i}-Y_{j}\|^{2}/2
. We define an estimator
\widehat{\gamma}
of the unbalanced optimal transport plan
\gamma
. For the Gaussian-Hellinger Kantorovich problem (
3
), one may take any solution of

\widehat{\gamma}\in\argmin_{\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n\times m}}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}C_{ij}\gamma_{ij}^{\prime}+D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\mid\widehat{\mu}_{n})+D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime}\mid\widehat{\nu}_{m})\right\}.

(8)

(ii) Estimate discrete transport:

Define the row masses
\widehat{r}_{i}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{\gamma}_{ij}
,
i=1,\dots,n
. We then define a point estimator of the transported component by the Fréchet projection: if
\widehat{r}_{i}>0
,

\widehat{T}_{i}=\argmin_{y\in\Omega}\ \sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{\gamma}_{ij}c(y,Y_{j})=\frac{1}{\widehat{r}_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{\gamma}_{ij}Y_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(9)

If
\widehat{r}_{i}=0
, then the above objective is identically zero and
\widehat{T}_{i}
is not identifiable from the plan. In this case, we set
\widehat{T}_{i}\coloneqq X_{i}
by convention.
Motivated by the active-marginal identity
\gamma_{0}=a_{0}^{2}\mu
from Section
2
, we define an estimator
\widehat{a}_{i}\coloneqq\sqrt{{\widehat{r}_{i}}/{\widehat{\mu}_{i}}}
for the active-source factor
a_{0}(X_{i})
.
To estimate the growth factor
\lambda_{0}(X_{i})
, we fix constants
0<w_{-}<w_{+}
containing the range of
w_{0}=e^{-\varphi_{0}}
and set

\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}_{i}\coloneqq\clip_{[w_{-},w_{+}]}(\widehat{a}_{i}^{2})\exp\left(\frac{1}{4}\|X_{i}-\widehat{T}_{i}\|^{2}\right).

(10)

(iii) Estimate Monge/growth map

Since
(\widehat{T}_{i},\widehat{a}_{i},\widehat{\lambda}_{i})
are defined only at the sample points
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
, we extend them to all of
\Omega
.
Here, we employ an approach of the nearest-neighbour method. Let
(V_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be the Voronoi partition induced by
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
:

V_{i}\coloneqq\{x\in\Omega:\ \|x-X_{i}\|\leq\|x-X_{k}\|\ \forall k\neq i\}
.
Setting
w_{i}(x)\coloneqq\mathbf{1}\{x\in V_{i}\}
yields the piecewise-constant estimators

\widehat{T}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{x\in V_{i}\}\widehat{T}_{i},~~\widehat{a}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{x\in V_{i}\}\widehat{a}_{i},~~\widehat{\lambda}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{x\in V_{i}\}\widehat{\lambda}_{i},

(11)

where
\widehat{a}^{\mathrm{1NN}}
and
\widehat{\lambda}^{\mathrm{1NN}}
estimate the active-source factor
a_{0}
and the growth map
\lambda_{0}
, respectively.

In addition to the nearest-neighbor method, a Nadaraya-Watson-type estimator can also be considered; see Definition
2
in Appendix
C.1
.

3.2.
Kernel-based estimator

We next present a kernel-based estimator for the transport-growth pair on the hypercube
\Omega=[0,1]^{d}
.
This estimator is based on density estimates of the measures
\mu,\nu
and has the advantage of adapting to the smoothness of these densities.

(i) Prepare kernels

:
We introduce a new kernel function based on a cosine basis for estimating density functions on
[0,1]^{d}
. This method makes statistical use
[
14
,
36
]
of the eigenfunctions of the Neumann Laplacian
[
34
]
. This kernel is useful to relax the constraint that a density function must lie on a torus, a requirement in the balanced case
[
24
]
.

We define the kernel function. In preparation, we define a function
\eta(s)=e^{-1/s}\mathbf{1}\{s>0\}
for
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\tau(t)\coloneqq{\eta(2-t)}/({\eta(2-t)+\eta(t-1)})
for
t\geq 0
.
Then,
\tau\in C^{\infty}([0,\infty))
,
0\leq\tau\leq 1
,
\tau(t)=1
for
t\in[0,1]
, and
\tau(t)=0
for
t\geq 2
.
With
c_{0}\coloneqq 1,c_{\ell}\coloneqq\sqrt{2}\ \ (\ell\geq 1)
,
and for
L\geq 1
, define the one-dimensional Neumann kernel

\displaystyle\kappa_{L}(u,v)\coloneqq 1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\tau\left(\frac{\pi^{2}\ell^{2}}{L^{2}}\right)c_{\ell}^{2}\cos(\pi\ell u)\cos(\pi\ell v),\qquad u,v\in[0,1].

(12)

Because
\tau
has compact support, the sum in (
12
) is finite for each
L
. We then define the boundary-adapted separable kernels

\displaystyle K_{L}(x,y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\prod_{r=1}^{d}\kappa_{L}(x_{r},y_{r}),\qquad x,y\in[0,1]^{d}.

(13)

Since each one-dimensional factor contains only
O(L)
nonzero cosine modes, one evaluation of
K_{L}(x,y)
costs
O(dL)
arithmetic operations. Moreover, we have
\int_{[0,1]^{d}}K_{L}(x,y)dy=1
for
x\in[0,1]^{d}
.
This is the cube analogue of a smooth spectral cutoff, but expressed in a form that is computationally tractable in moderate and high dimension: the cosine basis supplies the boundary adaptation, while the coordinatewise multiplier supplies exact separability.

(ii) Estimate measures via densities

:
We estimate the normalized densities first and then attach either the true masses (for the oracle equal-mass objects used in the analysis) or the estimated masses (for the actual fitted measures).
In particular, we define the preliminary kernel density estimators

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}_{n}^{\mathrm{ker}}(\cdot)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K_{L_{n}}(x,X_{i}),\qquad\widetilde{q}_{m}^{\mathrm{ker}}(\cdot)\coloneqq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}K_{L_{m}}(y,Y_{j}).

(14)

We then define estimator for the measures
\mu,\nu
with the estimated total mass as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}_{n}^{\mathrm{ker}}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\widehat{M}_{\mu}(\widetilde{p}_{n}^{\mathrm{ker}}(x))_{+}dx}{\int_{[0,1]^{d}}(\widetilde{p}_{n}^{\mathrm{ker}}(u))_{+}du},\qquad\widehat{\nu}_{m}^{\mathrm{ker}}(y)\coloneqq\frac{\widehat{M}_{\nu}(\widetilde{q}_{m}^{\mathrm{ker}}(y))_{+}dy}{\int_{[0,1]^{d}}(\widetilde{q}_{m}^{\mathrm{ker}}(v))_{+}dv}.

(15)

(iii) Estimate maps

:
Let
(\widehat{\varphi}_{nm}^{\mathrm{ker}},\widehat{\psi}_{nm}^{\mathrm{ker}})
be an optimal dual pair for

\mathrm{UOT}(\widehat{\mu}_{n}^{\mathrm{ker}},\widehat{\nu}_{m}^{\mathrm{ker}})
. We define the estimators of the active-source factor as
\widehat{a}_{nm}^{\mathrm{ker}}(x)\coloneqq e^{-\widehat{\varphi}_{nm}^{\mathrm{ker}}(x)/2}
.
Then, we define estimators for the transport map and the growth map by

\displaystyle\widehat{T}_{nm}^{\mathrm{ker}}(x)

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{y\in[0,1]^{d}}\left\{\|x-y\|^{2}/2-\widehat{\psi}_{nm}^{\mathrm{ker}}(y)\right\},

(16)

\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}_{nm}^{\mathrm{ker}}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\clip_{[w_{-},w_{+}]}(\widehat{a}_{nm}^{\mathrm{ker}}(x)^{2})\exp\left(\frac{1}{4}\|x-\widehat{T}_{nm}^{\mathrm{ker}}(x)\|^{2}\right).

(17)

4.
Minimax optimal rate

4.1.
Basic assumptions

Assumption 1
.

There exists a constant
\kappa\in(0,1)
such that

\left(1-\frac{1}{\kappa}\right)I\preceq\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0}(x)\preceq(1-\kappa)I,\qquad x\in\Omega.

The bounds in Assumption
1
are equivalent to
\kappa I\preceq\nabla^{2}f_{0}(x)\preceq\kappa^{-1}I
for the Brenier potential
f_{0}(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\|x\|^{2}-\varphi_{0}(x)
commonly used in convergence analysis of balanced OT map estimators
[
21
,
24
,
13
]
. The following consequence will be used regularly in our proofs:

Lemma 2
.

Suppose that Assumption
1
holds. Then the dual potentials
(\varphi_{0},\psi_{0})
satisfy:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}-\varphi_{0}(x)-\psi_{0}(y)\geq\frac{\kappa}{2}\|y-T_{0}(x)\|^{2},\qquad x,y\in\Omega.

(18)

Assumption 2
.

The domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is compact, convex, and satisfies the interior cone condition: there exist
\varepsilon_{0},\delta_{0}>0
such that for all
x\in\Omega
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,

\mathrm{Vol}(B(x,\varepsilon)\cap\Omega)\geq\delta_{0}\mathrm{Vol}(B(x,\varepsilon)).

Assumption 3
.

The positive finite measures
\mu
and
\nu
have total masses
M_{\mu}
and
M_{\nu}
. Their normalized laws
\bar{\mu}=\mu/M_{\mu}
and
\bar{\nu}=\nu/M_{\nu}
admit Lebesgue densities
p
and
q
bounded away from zero and infinity on
\Omega
:
0<\beta_{\min}\leq p(x),q(x)\leq\beta_{\max}<\infty
. Equivalently, the finite-measure densities are
\rho_{\mu}=M_{\mu}p
and
\rho_{\nu}=M_{\nu}q
.

The two assumptions are standard in the statistical literature on transport map estimation: similar assumptions on the densities, on the transport potential or on the map itself appear in the balanced case
[
21
,
24
,
3
]
and the unbalanced case
[
18
]
.

Assumption 4
.

There exist positive real sequences
a_{n},b_{m}\to 0
as
n,m\to\infty
and a constant
c\in(0,1]
such that

\widehat{M}_{\mu}\to M_{\mu},\widehat{M}_{\nu}\to M_{\nu},
and

\widehat{M}_{\mu}\geq cM_{\mu}
,
\widehat{M}_{\nu}\geq cM_{\nu}
, almost surely,
and
\mathbb{E}[|\widehat{M}_{\mu}-M_{\mu}|]\leq a_{n}
,
\mathbb{E}[|\widehat{M}_{\nu}-M_{\nu}|]\leq b_{m}
hold for all sufficiently large
n,m
.

This assumption is intentionally modular rather than standard in the UOT setup.
The condition itself is mild and it is satisfied, for example, when the masses are known, when separate total-mass measurements are available, or in point-process/counting models where the counts carry mass information
[
20
,
29
]
. For that reason we state it abstractly instead of tying the main theory to a single data-acquisition mechanism.

4.2.
Plan-based estimator

We study the estimation error of the
1
NN estimator (
11
). The transport loss is measured under the
active source measure

\gamma_{0}=a_{0}^{2}\mu
, which is the natural source marginal in the Kantorovich problem; the growth loss is measured for
\lambda_{0}
under the empirical source measure
\widehat{\mu}_{n}
.
In preparation, we define the following value:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{n}^{\mathrm{emp}}(d)\coloneqq\begin{cases}n^{-1/2},&d\leq 3,\\
(\log n)n^{-1/2},&d=4,\\
n^{-2/d},&d\geq 5,\end{cases}

(19)

which corresponds to the convergence rate of empirical distribution in the Wasserstein distance
W_{2}

[
39
]
.
Then, we obtain the following rate:

Theorem 3

(Error rate of plan-based estimator)
.

Assume that Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
hold.
Let
(\widehat{T}^{\mathrm{1NN}},\widehat{a}^{\mathrm{1NN}},\widehat{\lambda}^{\mathrm{1NN}})
be the
1
NN estimators defined in Section
3.1
, where
\widehat{\lambda}^{\mathrm{1NN}}
is the clipped growth estimator in (
10
). Let
a_{n},b_{m}
be the sequences from Assumption
4
.
Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that, for all sufficiently large
n,m
,

\displaystyle\max\left\{\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\int_{\Omega}\|\widehat{T}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(x)-T_{0}(x)\|^{2}d\mu(x)\Bigr],\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\int_{\Omega}|\widehat{\lambda}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(x)-\lambda_{0}(x)|^{2}d\mu(x)\Bigr]\right\}

(20)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\log n\Bigl(M_{\mu}\mathfrak{R}_{n}^{\mathrm{emp}}(d)+M_{\nu}\mathfrak{R}_{m}^{\mathrm{emp}}(d)+a_{n}+b_{m}\Bigr).

(21)

This result shows that the estimation error of an unbalanced mapping by 1NN, when excluding the effects of mass estimation
a_{n}
and
b_{m}
, is equal to the rate of convergence of the empirical distribution in the
W_{2}
.
Furthermore, since
a_{n}
and
b_{m}
achieve the parametric rate in many cases, they do not hamper the convergence rate of the empirical distribution.

4.3.
Kernel-based estimator on the hypercube

We study the estimation error of the kernel estimator (
16
) on
[0,1]^{d}
. Here, we additionally introduce an assumption on the smoothness of the normalized densities of
\mu,\nu
.

Definition 1

(Neumann-compatible Hölder class)
.

Let
\mathbb{T}_{2}^{d}\coloneqq(\mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z})^{d}
. For
f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, define its even
2
-periodic reflection
\mathcal{E}f:\mathbb{T}_{2}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by

(\mathcal{E}f)(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\coloneqq f\bigl(\vartheta(x_{1}),\dots,\vartheta(x_{d})\bigr)
, where
\vartheta(t)\coloneqq\min_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}|t-2m|\in[0,1]
.
For
s>0
and
M>0
, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{N}^{s}([0,1]^{d};M)\coloneqq\left\{f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}:\|\mathcal{E}f\|_{C^{s}(\mathbb{T}_{2}^{d})}\leq M\right\},

(22)

which consists of functions whose coordinatewise even reflection is
C^{s}
-smooth on the doubled torus.

Assumption 5

(Smooth density)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{N}^{s}([0,1]^{d};M)
be the Neumann-compatible Hölder class.
For some
\alpha>1
and
M>0
, the normalized densities satisfy
p,q\in\mathcal{C}_{N}^{\alpha-1}([0,1]^{d};M)
.

In preparation, we define a benchmark rate as follows, then obtain the result on the estimation error:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{n}^{\mathrm{ker}}(\alpha)\coloneqq\begin{cases}n^{-1},&d=1,\\
(\log n)n^{-1},&d=2,\\
n^{-{2\alpha}/({2(\alpha-1)+d})},&d\geq 3\end{cases}.

(23)

Theorem 4

(Error rate of kernel estimator)
.

Assume that
\Omega=[0,1]^{d}
, and Assumptions
1
,
3
and
4
, and
5
hold.
Assume that
L_{n}\asymp n^{1/(d+2(\alpha-1))}
and
L_{m}\asymp m^{1/(d+2(\alpha-1))}
, and let
a_{n},b_{m}
be the sequences from Assumption
4
.
Then there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on
d,M,\beta_{\min},\beta_{\max}
, the constants in Assumption
1
, and the cutoff
\tau
, such that

\displaystyle\max\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\|\widehat{T}_{nm}^{\mathrm{ker}}(x)-T_{0}(x)\|^{2}d\mu(x)\right],\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{[0,1]^{d}}|\widehat{\lambda}_{nm}^{\mathrm{ker}}(x)-\lambda_{0}(x)|^{2}d\mu(x)\right]\right\}

(24)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\Bigl(M_{\mu}\mathfrak{R}_{n}^{\mathrm{ker}}(\alpha)+M_{\nu}\mathfrak{R}_{m}^{\mathrm{ker}}(\alpha)+a_{n}+b_{m}\Bigr).

(25)

This result provides the following insights: (i) Under the smoothness assumption for the density function, the kernel estimator achieves a faster rate, exceeding the rate
O(n^{-2/d})
of the empirical estimator in Theorem
3
. This mitigates the curse of dimensionality in the rate of Theorem
3
through the smoothness. (ii) The kernel estimator enables the estimation of mappings on a hypercube
[0,1]^{d}
, which contrasts with the estimator in
[
24
]
, which performed estimation on a torus. (iii) This rate is minimax optimal up to a logarithmic factor, as we will see in the next section.

4.4.
Proof outline for the upper bounds: Stability approach

We present the case of the plan-based estimator below, while the approach is similar for the kernel-based estimator. Below,
a\lesssim b
means
a\leq Cb
for some constant
C>0
.

Let
\widehat{\gamma}
be the optimizer of
\mathrm{UOT}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m})
. Define the fitted row and column masses
\widehat{r}_{i}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{\gamma}_{ij}
and
\widehat{s}_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\gamma}_{ij}
, the oracle active masses
\widehat{r}^{\star}_{i}\coloneqq e^{-\varphi_{0}(X_{i})}\widehat{\mu}_{i}
and
\widehat{s}^{\star}_{j}\coloneqq e^{-\psi_{0}(Y_{j})}\widehat{\nu}_{j}
, the barycentric projections
\widehat{T}_{i}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\widehat{r}_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{\gamma}_{ij}Y_{j}
, and the barycentric error
\widehat{\Delta}_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{r}_{i}\|\widehat{T}_{i}-T_{0}(X_{i})\|^{2}
. A key ingredient of the proof is the following bound, which relates the risk under the population active measure
\gamma_{0}\coloneqq e^{-\varphi_{0}}\mu
to the discrete UOT solution and the Voronoi geometry (Theorem
20
):

\int_{\Omega}\|\widehat{T}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(x)-T_{0}(x)\|^{2}d\gamma_{0}(x)\lesssim nM_{n}\widehat{\Delta}_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widehat{r}\mid\widehat{r}^{\star})+R_{n}^{2},

(26)

Here,
M_{n}
and
R_{n}
denote the maximum Voronoi cell mass and radius. By the Vapnik-Chervonenkis inequality (Lemma
18
), we have
M_{n}\lesssim\log n/n
and
\mathbb{E}[R_{n}^{2}]\lesssim(\log n/n)^{2/d}
with high probability.

To bound
\widehat{\Delta}_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}
and
{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widehat{r}\mid\widehat{r}^{\star})
, we consider the empirical excess
E_{n}\coloneqq\mathrm{UOT}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m})-\int(1-e^{-\varphi_{0}(x)})d\widehat{\mu}_{n}-\int(1-e^{-\psi_{0}(y)})d\widehat{\nu}_{m}
. We show that it can be expressed as follows (Proposition
17
):

\displaystyle E_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{\gamma}_{ij}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\|X_{i}-Y_{j}\|^{2}-\varphi_{0}(X_{i})-\psi_{0}(Y_{j})\Bigr)+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widehat{r}\mid\widehat{r}^{\star})+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widehat{s}\mid\widehat{s}^{\star}).

(27)

By dual feasibility, all terms on the right-hand side are nonnegative. Hence,
{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widehat{r}\mid\widehat{r}^{\star})\leq E_{n}
. By Jensen’s inequality and Lemma
2
, we have
\widehat{\Delta}_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{\gamma}_{ij}\|Y_{j}-T_{0}(X_{i})\|^{2}\leq\frac{2}{\kappa}E_{n}
.

Taking the expectation, we then employ our stability bound (Proposition
10
), which states:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[E_{n}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{UOT}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m})]-\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}[W_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\mu)]+\mathbb{E}[W_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\nu}_{m},\nu)]+a_{n}+b_{m},

(28)

where
a_{n}
and
b_{m}
are the sequences from Assumption
4
. Using any existing bound on the Wasserstein distances of the empirical measures, e.g.,
[
16
]
, we achieve the final bound (
25
).

To bound the growth risk of
\widehat{\lambda}^{\mathrm{1NN}}
, we relate the empirical growth map error to the active-source factor error and the transport map error using the following bound (Lemma
22
):

|\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-\lambda_{0}(X_{i})|^{2}\leq|\widehat{a}_{i}-a_{0}(X_{i})|^{2}+\|\widehat{T}_{i}-T_{0}(X_{i})\|^{2},

(29)

where
\widehat{a}_{i}\coloneqq\sqrt{\widehat{r}_{i}/\widehat{\mu}_{i}}
is a plug-in estimate of the active-source factor. Multiplying both sides by
\widehat{\mu}_{i}
and summing over
i
, we show in Lemma
25
that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\mu}_{i}|\widehat{a}_{i}-a_{0}(X_{i})|^{2}\leq{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widehat{r}\mid\widehat{r}^{\star})
. The rest of the proof proceeds analogously to that of the transport map.

4.5.
Minimax lower bound on the hypercube

To match Theorem
4
with a lower bound in the same smooth hypercube regime, we restrict to
\Omega=[0,1]^{d}
, assume that the masses
M_{\mu},M_{\nu}
are known, and the sample sizes are equal:
m=n
.
We write
\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\nu}^{n}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\nu}^{n}
for the joint law and expectation, respectively.

Fix
\alpha>1
and parameters
M,B<\infty
and
\Lambda>1
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}(M,B,\Lambda)
denote the class of pairs
(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,1]^{d})^{2}
such that Assumptions
1
,
3
, and
5
hold, with smoothness radius
M
in Assumption
5
; the unique
c
-concave solution
\varphi_{0}
of the dual problem (
4
) and the associated solution
(T_{0},\lambda_{0})
of the Monge problem (
2
) obtained via Theorem
1
satisfy
\|T_{0}\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,1]^{d})}+\|\lambda_{0}\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,1]^{d})}\leq B
and
\Lambda^{-1}\leq\lambda_{0}(x)\leq\Lambda
for all
x\in[0,1]^{d}
.

Theorem 5

(Minimax lower bound in the smooth hypercube regime)
.

For every
\alpha>1
, there exist constants
M_{0},B_{0}<\infty
and
\Lambda_{0}>1
such that, for every
M\geq M_{0}
,
B\geq B_{0}
, and
\Lambda\geq\Lambda_{0}
, there is a constant
c_{0}>0
, depending only on
d,\alpha,M,B,\Lambda
and the constants in Assumptions
1
and
3
, such that for all sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}(M,B,\Lambda)}\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\nu}^{n}\left[\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\|\widehat{T}(x)-T_{0}(x)\|^{2}d\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle\geq c_{0}\left(n^{-2\alpha/(2\alpha-2+d)}\vee n^{-1}\right),

(30)

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\lambda}}\sup_{(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}(M,B,\Lambda)}\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\nu}^{n}\left[\int_{[0,1]^{d}}|\widehat{\lambda}(x)-\lambda_{0}(x)|^{2}d\mu(x)\right]

\displaystyle\geq c_{0}\left(n^{-2\alpha/(2\alpha-2+d)}\vee n^{-1}\right),

(31)

where the infima are taken over all measurable estimators of
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n},M_{\mu},M_{\nu})
.

The construction in the appendix lies inside the same regime as Theorem
4
: the potential
\varphi_{0}
is strongly convex, the associated transport map
T_{0}
and growth map
\lambda_{0}
have uniform
C^{\alpha}
bounds, the density functions are bounded above and away from zero. Hence, in view of Theorem
4
, our lower bound indicates that our kernel-based estimator is minimax optimal up to the logarithmic factor.

5.
Experiments

5.1.
Simulation study

(a)

(b)

Figure 1
.
(a) MSE of the four UOT estimators. Each plot shows the average over 10 seeds with one standard error. Top: The MSEs of estimating
T_{0}
and
\lambda_{0}
vs
n
. Bottom: Learning rates for
T_{0}
and
\lambda_{0}
vs.
d
. (b) Top: Incomplete 3D shapes. Middle: Complete 3D shapes predicted by the plan-based 1NN estimator. Bottom: Complete 3D shapes predicted by the plan-based kernel estimator.

We sample data from source and target measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,1]^{d})
whose densities are
1
-Hölder smooth, with
\mu([0,1]^{d})=1
and
\nu([0,1]^{d})=2.5
. We benchmark several estimators of the oracle pair
(T_{0},\lambda_{0})
, namely plan-based 1NN (PB-1NN), plan-based kernel (PB-Kernel), plug-in kernel (PI-Kernel), and plug-in wavelet (PI-Wavelet cf. Appendix
G
). We also include the SSUOT estimator proposed by
[
38
]
as a competing baseline. Additional details are provided in Appendix
K.2
.

Figure
1(a)
shows MSEs and empirical learning rates of the five methods. Specifically, the two learning rate plots (the bottom row) indicate that PB-1NN, PB-Kernel and SSUOT’s learning rates for
T_{0}
and
\lambda_{0}
are faster than both theoretical upper bound of
n^{-0.5}
in Theorem
3
, while the learning rates of our plug-in estimators closely match the theoretical upper bound in Theorem
4
(the dashed line). Moreover, the two MSE plots (the top row) indicate that our plug-in kernel and wavelet estimators are more accurate and yield faster learning rates than the plan-based estimators and SSUOT.

5.2.
Application to 3D shape completion

To showcase our plan-based estimators for high-dimensional tasks, we apply them to an unpaired 3D shape completion task using the Completion3D dataset
[
6
,
40
]
. Specifically, we use the set of incomplete 3D point clouds of chairs as the source dataset and their complete versions as the target dataset. To test robustness, we introduce 30 incomplete cars into the source dataset as outliers. The point clouds are first transformed into a
16\times 16\times 16
grid and subsequently projected via PCA into 1024 dimensions, yielding a source-target dataset pair with
n=5780
,
m=5750
, and
d=1024
. In this task, both source and target measures have unit mass. Nonetheless, we can leverage UOT to selectively reject outliers through the growth map assignment.

We fit PB-1NN and PB-Kernel to this dataset pair and use them to predict the complete shapes of four chairs and four cars in a held-out test set. As Figure
1(b)
illustrates, both estimators successfully recover the shapes of the test chairs. However, their behavior diverges on the outlier test cars: while PB-1NN attempts to reconstruct chairs from the cars, PB-Kernel predicts the mean in the latent PCA space and yields values of
\widehat{\lambda}
that are close to zero. This indicates PB-Kernel implicitly discards the outliers, demonstrating that it can perform unpaired 3D shape completion in the presence of outliers.

6.
Conclusion

We studied statistical estimation in unbalanced optimal transport with quadratic cost, where the target estimand consists of the transport map and the growth map. We developed the plan-based estimator and the kernel-based estimator, then derive convergence rates of their estimation errors. We also showed the optimality of the kernel-based estimator by deriving the lower bound. Main technical contribution is a reduction of the estimation to the stability bound, and proving the bound itself. Overall, our results provide a statistical foundation for estimation in UOT and clarify the distinct roles of active marginals, growth estimation, and first-order bias in this problem.
A limitation is that our setup does not cover entropic regularization, and this is an interesting future work.

Appendix A
Related work

Foundations of unbalanced transport.

Modern UOT is largely built on entropy-transport formulations, which relax the marginal constraints by convex divergences and recover important geometries such as logarithmic entropy-transport and Hellinger-Kantorovich/Wasserstein-Fisher-Rao
[
9
,
22
]
. Recent work has clarified the Monge viewpoint, primal-dual optimality conditions, and regularity theory for these models
[
32
,
18
]
. These results provide the structural background for our Monge-type analysis.

Statistical map estimation in balanced OT.

In the balanced setting, OT map estimation is now supported by a fairly rich statistical theory. Minimax-optimal rates for smooth maps were established via semi-dual curvature arguments by
[
21
]
. On the plug-in side, barycentric-projection estimators based on empirical or smoothed couplings were analyzed by
[
12
]
, while entropic estimators were developed by
[
28
]
and shown to be particularly effective in semi-discrete and discontinuous settings by
[
25
]
. Sharp smooth plug-in guarantees were obtained by
[
24
]
, and the stability-based perspective has recently been broadened both to more general function classes and to sharper reductions from map estimation to distribution estimation
[
13
,
3
,
26
]
. Our work can be viewed as an unbalanced counterpart to this stability/plugin line, but with a different target: the transport-growth pair induced by the active marginals of the UOT plan.

Statistical theory for UOT

The closest prior works are those of
[
37
,
38
]
, which derive semi-dual formulations and global Bregman-type stability bounds for quadratic UOT, leading to the first fast rates for UOT semi-dual potentials. Our contribution differs in both target and technique: rather than estimating a potential under a semi-dual metric, we study the population Monge-type transport-growth pair
(T_{0},\lambda_{0})
and relate its risk directly to perturbations of the UOT value and to Wasserstein/
L^{1}
errors of fitted measures. Rigorous statistical results for other UOT models remain scarce. In particular,
[
20
]
study unbalanced Kantorovich-Rubinstein distances, plans, and barycenters on finite spaces, and
[
29
]
analyze sharp rates for empirical unbalanced KR quantities in spatio-temporal point-process models. These works address different costs, observation models, and statistical targets from the Monge-type estimation problem considered here.

Algorithms and applications.

A large parallel literature develops scalable algorithms and applications for UOT; see
[
30
]
for a broad overview. On the computational side, generalized Sinkhorn methods and their complexity or acceleration for unbalanced problems were studied by
[
27
,
35
]
, and lightweight or neural parameterizations have been proposed for large-scale settings
[
17
,
41
,
4
]
. On the application side, UOT and WFR-type models have been used in single-cell trajectory inference and population dynamics
[
33
,
31
]
, tumor-growth and reaction-diffusion models
[
11
]
, gradient-flow learning
[
42
]
, and geometric shape analysis
[
2
]
. These works strongly motivate statistical guarantees for UOT, but they do not provide a nonparametric estimation theory for Monge-type UOT maps.

Appendix B
Proof outline for kernel-based estimator

The proof of Theorem
4
follows the same abstract stability principle as the proof of Theorem
3
, but with the empirical measures
\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m}
replaced by smooth fitted measures
\widehat{\mu}_{n}^{\mathrm{ker}}
and
\widehat{\nu}_{m}^{\mathrm{ker}}
.

(i) Stability argument

: Lemma
35
shows that if one solves UOT between fitted measures
(\widehat{\mu}_{n}^{\mathrm{ker}},\widehat{\nu}_{m}^{\mathrm{ker}})
, then the empirical UOT cost decomposes into the same three pieces as in the plan-based case: a map-mismatch term and two
D_{\mathrm{KL}}
penalties against the oracle active marginals
e^{-\varphi_{0}}\widehat{\mu}_{n}^{\mathrm{ker}}
and
e^{-\psi_{0}}\widehat{\nu}_{m}^{\mathrm{ker}}
. Consequently, the transport map risk and the growth-factor risk are reduced to controlling
W_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\mu}_{n}^{\mathrm{ker}},\mu)
,
W_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\nu}_{m}^{\mathrm{ker}},\nu)
, and the corresponding
L^{1}
errors.

(ii) Analysis of the kernel

:
The estimator-specific step is the analysis of the designed kernel function. Proposition
29
proves the one-sample bounds for the fitted kernel measures. Informally, for the resolution choice
L_{n}\asymp n^{1/(d+2(\alpha-1))}
one obtains

\mathbb{E}\bigl[W_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\mu}_{n}^{\mathrm{ker}},\mu)\bigr]+\mathbb{E}\bigl[W_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\nu}_{m}^{\mathrm{ker}},\nu)\bigr]\lesssim\mathfrak{R}_{n}^{\mathrm{ker}}(\alpha)+\mathfrak{R}_{m}^{\mathrm{ker}}(\alpha)+a_{n}+b_{m},

together with matching
L^{1}
bounds. The nontrivial point here is the boundary: the estimator cannot use the Fourier kernel directly because the cube densities are not periodic. Instead, we work in the cosine basis, which corresponds to smoothing the even reflection on the doubled torus and preserves the boundary. The coordinatewise multiplier is chosen so that the kernel factorizes exactly into one-dimensional sums, avoiding an
O(L^{d})
summation over multi-indices.
Finally, once the estimated measures satisfy the required
W_{2}^{2}
and
L^{1}
rates, combining them to the stability lemmas yields the statement.

Appendix C
Additional constructions and remarks

C.1.
Nadaraya-Watson extension of the discrete plan-based estimator

Definition 2

(Nadaraya-Watson extension)
.

Let
(\widehat{T}_{i},\widehat{a}_{i},\widehat{\lambda}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be the discrete quantities defined in (
9
), (
10
) and their around. Fix a nonnegative kernel
K:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
and a bandwidth
h>0
. For
x\in\Omega
, define the normalized weights

\displaystyle w_{i,h}(x)\coloneqq\frac{K\left((x-X_{i})/h\right)}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}K\left((x-X_{k})/h\right)},

whenever the denominator is positive. The associated Nadaraya–Watson extension is then given by

\displaystyle\widehat{T}_{h}^{\mathrm{NW}}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i,h}(x)\widehat{T}_{i},\quad\widehat{a}_{h}^{\mathrm{NW}}(x)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i,h}(x)\widehat{a}_{i},\quad\widehat{\lambda}_{h}^{\mathrm{NW}}(x)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i,h}(x)\widehat{\lambda}_{i}.

The second display smooths the auxiliary active-source factor, whereas the third smooths the already corrected Gaussian–Hellinger growth estimator.

This is the standard kernel-smoothing analogue of the
1
NN extension. As in the main text, the smoothed fields need not preserve the exact empirical marginal constraint, but they provide continuous alternatives when the kernel and the bandwidth are chosen suitably.

C.2.
Obtaining samples through Poisson point processes

We consider that the observations follows two independent Poisson point processes as

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}=\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{\mu}}\sim\mathrm{PPP}(\mu),\qquad\mathcal{Y}=\{Y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N_{\nu}}\sim\mathrm{PPP}(\nu).

(32)

Here, the sample sizes satisfy
N_{\mu}\sim\mathrm{Poisson}(M_{\mu})
and
N_{\nu}\sim\mathrm{Poisson}(M_{\nu})
.
Conditional on
N_{\mu}
,
X_{i}
independently and identically follows
(M_{\mu})^{-1}\mu
.
Then, we define an empirical measure

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mu}}\delta_{X_{i}}\qquad\widehat{\nu}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\nu}}\delta_{Y_{j}}.

(33)

In this setup, we have
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\mu}]=\mu
. This setup with slight modification is studied by
[
29
]
.

C.3.
Proof of Lemma
2

We proof Lemma
2
that allows us to convert the constraint on the Hessian of the UOT potential into a lower bound for the gap in the constraint
\varphi_{0}(x)+\psi_{0}(y)\leq\frac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}
.

Lemma 6

(Lemma
2
, restated)
.

Suppose that Assumption
1
holds. Then the dual potentials satisfy the following uniform lower bound on the gap:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}-\varphi_{0}(x)-\psi_{0}(y)\geq\frac{\kappa}{2}\|y-T_{0}(x)\|^{2},\qquad x,y\in\Omega.

(34)

Proof.

Define the associated Brenier potential
f_{0}(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\|x\|^{2}-\varphi_{0}(x)
. The lower bound in Assumption
1
, namely
\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0}(x)\succeq(1-\kappa^{-1})I
, implies that the Hessian of the Brenier potential obeys the uniform upper bound:

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}f_{0}(x)=I-\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0}(x)\preceq\frac{1}{\kappa}I.

(35)

Let
f_{0}^{*}(y)\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}\|y\|^{2}-\psi_{0}(y)
be the Legendre-Fenchel conjugate of
f_{0}(x)
. Since the Hessian matrix of
f^{*}_{0}
is the inverse of the Hessian matrix of
f_{0}
, the hypothesis
\nabla^{2}f_{0}(x)\preceq\kappa^{-1}I
implies

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}f_{0}^{*}(y)=I-\nabla^{2}\psi_{0}(y)\succeq\kappa I.

(36)

For each fixed
x\in\Omega
, define

\displaystyle g_{x}(y)\coloneqq c(x,y)-\psi_{0}(y)=\tfrac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}-\psi_{0}(y).

(37)

Its Hessian is

\nabla_{y}^{2}g_{x}(y)=I-\nabla^{2}\psi_{0}(y)\succeq\kappa I,

so
g_{x}
is
\kappa
-strongly convex in
y
.
By the
c
-transform relation,

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}(x)=\inf_{y\in\Omega}\bigl\{c(x,y)-\psi_{0}(y)\bigr\}=g_{x}(T_{0}(x)),

(38)

hence
T_{0}(x)
minimizes
g_{x}
.
Strong convexity therefore implies

\displaystyle g_{x}(y)-g_{x}(T_{0}(x))\geq\frac{\kappa}{2}\|y-T_{0}(x)\|^{2},

(39)

which is exactly the desired inequality.
∎

Appendix D
Stability Bound

We recall two extensions of the empirical measures
\widehat{\mu}_{n}
and
\widehat{\nu}_{m}
.
Their normalized empirical probability measures are defined as

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}_{n}\coloneqq{\widehat{\mu}_{n}/\widehat{M}_{\mu}}={1/n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}},\qquad\bar{\nu}_{m}\coloneqq{\widehat{\nu}_{m}/\widehat{M}_{\nu}}={1/m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{Y_{j}}.

(40)

We also define the weighted empirical measure with the total mass
M_{\mu}
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{n}\coloneqq{M_{\mu}/n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}=M_{\mu}\bar{\mu}_{n},\qquad\widetilde{\nu}_{m}\coloneqq{M_{\nu}/m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\delta_{Y_{j}}=M_{\nu}\bar{\nu}_{m}.

(41)

Note that these measure has the same total mass of
\mu
and
\nu
, i.e.,
\widetilde{\mu}_{n}(\Omega)=\mu(\Omega)=M_{\mu}
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{m}(\Omega)=\nu(\Omega)=M_{\nu}
hold.

Throughout the proof, we denote
c(x,y)=\tfrac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}
.

D.1.
Stability bound with known masses

We are now ready to state the two-sample stability bound for the UOT problem with
\widetilde{\mu}_{n}
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{m}
, in the case that the masses
M_{\mu}
and
M_{\nu}
are known.

Proposition 7

(Two-sample stability of
\mathrm{UOT}
)
.

Let
\mu,\nu,\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)
. Consider the UOT model introduced in Section
2
. Let
\varphi_{0},\psi_{0}
be the optimal dual potentials for
(\mu,\nu)
. Assume that the problem setting satisfies Assumptions
1
-
3
. Then there exists a constant
C_{\Lambda}
such that:

\displaystyle\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})-\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)

(42)

\displaystyle\leq\int\zeta_{0}d(\widetilde{\mu}_{n}-\mu)+\int\xi_{0}d(\widetilde{\nu}_{m}-\nu)+C_{\Lambda}\left(M_{\mu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\mu}_{n},\bar{\mu})+M_{\nu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\nu}_{m},\bar{\nu})\right).

(43)

Proof of Proposition
7
.

Let
\gamma
be the optimal continuous coupling for
(\mu,\nu)
. Since
\mu
and
\nu
are both absolutely continuous (Assumption
3
), Brenier’s theorem ensures the existence of optimal transport maps
T_{\mu}:\Omega\to\Omega
and
T_{\nu}:\Omega\to\Omega
such that
(T_{\mu})_{\#}\mu=\widetilde{\mu}_{n}
,
(T_{\nu})_{\#}\nu=\widetilde{\nu}_{m}
,
\int\|T_{\mu}(x)-x\|^{2}d\mu(x)=W_{2}^{2}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\mu)=M_{\mu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\mu}_{n},\bar{\mu})
, and
\int\|T_{\nu}(y)-y\|^{2}d\nu(y)=W_{2}^{2}(\widetilde{\nu}_{m},\nu)=M_{\nu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\nu}_{m},\bar{\nu})
. Define the candidate coupling
\widehat{\gamma}=(T_{\mu},T_{\nu})_{\#}\gamma
.
By the data-processing inequality,
D_{\mathrm{KL}}
contracts under deterministic maps:

{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widehat{\gamma}_{0}\mid\widetilde{\mu}_{n})={D_{\mathrm{KL}}}((T_{\mu})_{\#}\gamma_{0}\mid(T_{\mu})_{\#}\mu)\leq{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\gamma_{0}\mid\mu).

Similarly,
{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1}\mid\widetilde{\nu}_{m})\leq{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\gamma_{1}\mid\nu)
. Thus, the UOT objective for the candidate
\widehat{\gamma}
is bounded:

\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})\leq\int cd\widehat{\gamma}+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widehat{\gamma}_{0}\mid\widetilde{\mu}_{n})+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1}\mid\widetilde{\nu}_{m})\leq\int cd\widehat{\gamma}+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\gamma_{0}\mid\mu)+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\gamma_{1}\mid\nu).

Subtracting this inequality by
\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)=\int cd\gamma+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\gamma_{0}\mid\mu)+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\gamma_{1}\mid\nu)
, we get:

\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})-\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)\leq\int cd\widehat{\gamma}-\int cd\gamma.

By Lemma
13
, we have

\displaystyle\int cd\widehat{\gamma}-\int cd\gamma

\displaystyle\leq\int\|T_{\mu}(x)-x\|^{2}d\gamma_{0}(x)+\int\|T_{\nu}(y)-y\|^{2}d\gamma_{1}(y)

(44)

\displaystyle+\int\langle T_{\mu}(x)-x,x-y\rangle d\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle+\int\langle T_{\nu}(y)-y,y-x\rangle d\gamma(x,y).

First, we bound the two terms that involve
T_{\mu}(x)
.
By Assumption
1
, the dual potentials are bounded, so
M_{\varphi}\coloneqq\sup_{x\in\Omega}e^{-\varphi_{0}(x)}<\infty
and
M_{\psi}\coloneqq\sup_{y\in\Omega}e^{-\psi_{0}(y)}<\infty
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\int\|T_{\mu}(x)-x\|^{2}d\gamma_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\int\|T_{\mu}(x)-x\|^{2}e^{-\varphi_{0}(x)}d\mu(x)

(45)

\displaystyle\leq M_{\varphi}\int\|T_{\mu}(x)-x\|^{2}d\mu(x)=M_{\varphi}M_{\mu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\mu}_{n},\bar{\mu}).

(46)

For the other term, we use the first-order optimality relation

\displaystyle x-y=\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)\qquad\text{for $\gamma$-a.e.\ }(x,y),

which follows from
c(x,y)=\tfrac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}
and the complementary slackness for the
UOT dual: for
\gamma
-a.e.
(x,y)
,
\varphi_{0}(x)+\psi_{0}(y)=c(x,y)
, while

\varphi_{0}(x^{\prime})+\psi_{0}(y)\leq c(x^{\prime},y)
for all
x^{\prime}\in\Omega
. Differentiating in
x

at points where
\varphi_{0}
is differentiable (which holds
\mu
-a.e., hence

\gamma_{0}
-a.e. since
\gamma_{0}\ll\mu
, by Theorem
1

under Assumption
1
) yields
\nabla c(x,y)=\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)
,
i.e.,
x-y=\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\int\langle T_{\mu}(x)-x,x-y\rangle d\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int\langle T_{\mu}(x)-x,\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)\rangle d\gamma_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\int\langle T_{\mu}(x)-x,\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)\rangle e^{-\varphi_{0}(x)}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int\langle T_{\mu}(x)-x,\nabla\zeta_{0}(x)\rangle d\mu(x),

because
\nabla\zeta_{0}(x)=e^{-\varphi_{0}(x)}\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)
.
Since both
\nabla\zeta_{0}
and
\nabla\xi_{0}
are Lipschitz on
\Omega
by Lemma
12
, by taking

\Lambda\coloneqq\max\bigl(\mathrm{Lip}(\nabla\zeta_{0}),\mathrm{Lip}(\nabla\xi_{0})\bigr)>0
,
we have the following inequality for all
x,y\in\Omega
:

\displaystyle\bigl|\zeta_{0}(y)-\zeta_{0}(x)-\langle\nabla\zeta_{0}(x),y-x\rangle\bigr|\leq\frac{\Lambda}{2}\|y-x\|^{2}.

Applying this with
y=T_{\mu}(x)
yields

\displaystyle\langle\nabla\zeta_{0}(x),T_{\mu}(x)-x\rangle\leq\zeta_{0}(T_{\mu}(x))-\zeta_{0}(x)+\frac{\Lambda}{2}\|T_{\mu}(x)-x\|^{2}.

Integrating with respect to
\mu
and using
(T_{\mu})_{\#}\mu=\widetilde{\mu}_{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int\langle T_{\mu}(x)-x,\nabla\zeta_{0}(x)\rangle d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int(\zeta_{0}(T_{\mu}(x))-\zeta_{0}(x))d\mu(x)+\frac{\Lambda}{2}\int\|T_{\mu}(x)-x\|^{2}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int\zeta_{0}d(\widetilde{\mu}_{n}-\mu)+\frac{\Lambda}{2}M_{\mu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\mu}_{n},\bar{\mu}).

The terms in (
44
) that involve
T_{\nu}(y)
are handled in the same way, using
y-x=\nabla\psi_{0}(y)
for
\gamma
-a.e.
(x,y)
and noting that
\nabla\xi_{0}
is also
\Lambda
-Lipschitz by our choice of
\Lambda
.
Combining both bounds, we may take

\displaystyle C_{\Lambda}\coloneqq\max(M_{\varphi},M_{\psi})+\frac{\Lambda}{2}.

This proves (
42
).
∎

D.2.
Extension to estimated masses

We now remove the assumption that the empirical measures have exactly the same total masses as the target measures. We have
\widehat{\mu}_{n}=\alpha_{n}\widetilde{\mu}_{n}
and
\widehat{\nu}_{m}=\beta_{m}\widetilde{\nu}_{m},

where
\alpha_{n}\coloneqq{\widehat{M}_{\mu}}/{M_{\mu}}
and
\beta_{m}\coloneqq{\widehat{M}_{\nu}}/{M_{\nu}}
. Our bound relies on the following assumption that the masses are estimable:

In preparation, we study the fixed
D_{\mathrm{KL}}
-penalized formulation.
Since
\widehat{\mu}_{n}
and
\widetilde{\mu}_{n}
have the same support and differ only by a global multiplicative factor, the change in the objective can be controlled directly through the
D_{\mathrm{KL}}
terms.

Lemma 8

(
D_{\mathrm{KL}}
under measure scaling)
.

Let
\eta,\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)
with
\eta\ll\mu
, write
M_{\mu}\coloneqq\mu(\Omega)
and let
\alpha>0
. Then

\displaystyle{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\eta\mid\alpha\mu)={D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\eta\mid\mu)-\eta(\Omega)\log\alpha+(\alpha-1)M_{\mu}.

(47)

Proof of Lemma
8
.

Write
\eta=f\mu
. Then
\eta=(f/\alpha)(\alpha\mu)
, and therefore

\displaystyle{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\eta\mid\alpha\mu)

\displaystyle=\int\left(\frac{f}{\alpha}\log\frac{f}{\alpha}-\frac{f}{\alpha}+1\right)\mathrm{d}(\alpha\mu)

\displaystyle=\int\bigl(f\log f-f+1\bigr)\mathrm{d}\mu-(\log\alpha)\int f\mathrm{d}\mu+(\alpha-1)M_{\mu}

\displaystyle={D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\eta\mid\mu)-\eta(\Omega)\log\alpha+(\alpha-1)M_{\mu}.

∎

Lemma 9

(Bound on
\gamma(\Omega\times\Omega)
)
.

Let
\eta,\sigma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)
with
\eta(\Omega)=A>0
and
\sigma(\Omega)=B>0
, and let
\gamma
be any optimal plan for
\mathrm{UOT}(\eta,\sigma)
with quadratic cost. Then

\displaystyle\gamma(\Omega\times\Omega)\leq e\sqrt{AB}.

(48)

Proof of Lemma
9
.

Let
s\coloneqq\gamma(\Omega\times\Omega)=\gamma_{0}(\Omega)=\gamma_{1}(\Omega)
. Since the zero plan is feasible, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{UOT}(\eta,\sigma)\leq\eta(\Omega)+\sigma(\Omega)=A+B.

(49)

Because the transport cost is nonnegative, it follows that

\displaystyle{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\gamma_{0}\mid\eta)+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\gamma_{1}\mid\sigma)\leq A+B.

(50)

Now let
f=\mathrm{d}\gamma_{0}/\mathrm{d}\eta
. Since
t\mapsto t\log t-t+1
is convex, Jensen’s inequality yields

\displaystyle{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\gamma_{0}\mid\eta)

\displaystyle=\int\bigl(f\log f-f+1\bigr)\mathrm{d}\eta

(51)

\displaystyle\geq A\left(\frac{s}{A}\log\frac{s}{A}-\frac{s}{A}+1\right)=s\log\frac{s}{A}-s+A.

(52)

Similarly,

\displaystyle{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\gamma_{1}\mid\sigma)\geq s\log\frac{s}{B}-s+B.

(53)

Summing the last two inequalities and using the upper bound
A+B
gives

\displaystyle s\log\frac{s^{2}}{AB}-2s+A+B\leq A+B,

(54)

that is,

\displaystyle s\left(\log\frac{s^{2}}{AB}-2\right)\leq 0.

(55)

If
s=0
there is nothing to prove. Otherwise
\log(s^{2}/(AB))\leq 2
, hence
s\leq e\sqrt{AB}
.
∎

Proposition 10

(Stability of
\mathrm{UOT}
under estimated masses)
.

Assume that Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
, and
4
hold.
Let
C_{\Lambda}
be the stability constant from Proposition
7
.
Then, almost surely,

\displaystyle\mathrm{UOT}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m})-\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\int\zeta_{0}\mathrm{d}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n}-\mu)+\int\xi_{0}\mathrm{d}(\widetilde{\nu}_{m}-\nu)

(56)

\displaystyle+C_{\Lambda}\Bigl(M_{\mu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\mu}_{n},\bar{\mu})+M_{\nu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\nu}_{m},\bar{\nu})\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\Bigl(M_{\mu}|\alpha_{n}-1|+M_{\nu}|\beta_{m}-1|+e\sqrt{M_{\mu}M_{\nu}}\bigl(|\log\alpha_{n}|+|\log\beta_{m}|\bigr)\Bigr).

Consequently, there exists a constant
C_{M_{\mu},M_{\nu}}>0
such that, for all sufficiently large
n,m
, the following bound holds:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{UOT}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m})\bigr]-\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Lambda}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}[M_{\mu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\mu}_{n},\bar{\mu})]+\mathbb{E}[M_{\nu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\nu}_{m},\bar{\nu})]\Bigr)

(57)

\displaystyle+C_{M_{\mu},M_{\nu}}\bigl(a_{n}+b_{m}\bigr),

where
a_{n},b_{m}
are the rates from Assumption
4
.
In particular, if the right-hand side of Proposition
7
applied to
(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})
converges to
0
and Assumption
4
holds, then, we obtain the following convergence in probability:

\displaystyle\mathrm{UOT}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m})-\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)\to 0.

(58)

Proof of Proposition
10
.

Let
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m}
be an optimal plan for
\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})
, and define

s_{n,m}\coloneqq\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m}(\Omega\times\Omega)
.
Since we have
\widehat{\mu}_{n}=\alpha_{n}\widetilde{\mu}_{n}
and
\widehat{\nu}_{m}=\beta_{m}\widetilde{\nu}_{m}
, the same plan
\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m}
is admissible for
\mathrm{UOT}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m})
. Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{UOT}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m})

\displaystyle\leq\int c\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m}+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}((\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m})_{0}\mid\widehat{\mu}_{n})+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}((\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m})_{1}\mid\widehat{\nu}_{m}).

Applying Lemma
8
with
\eta=(\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m})_{0}
and
\mu=\widetilde{\mu}_{n}
, and then with
\eta=(\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m})_{1}
and
\mu=\widetilde{\nu}_{m}
, yields

\displaystyle{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}((\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m})_{0}\mid\widehat{\mu}_{n})

\displaystyle={D_{\mathrm{KL}}}((\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m})_{0}\mid\widetilde{\mu}_{n})-s_{n,m}\log\alpha_{n}+(\alpha_{n}-1)M_{\mu},

\displaystyle{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}((\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m})_{1}\mid\widehat{\nu}_{m})

\displaystyle={D_{\mathrm{KL}}}((\widetilde{\gamma}_{n,m})_{1}\mid\widetilde{\nu}_{m})-s_{n,m}\log\beta_{m}+(\beta_{m}-1)M_{\nu}.

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathrm{UOT}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m})-\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})\leq\Bigl(-s_{n,m}(\log\alpha_{n}+\log\beta_{m})+(\alpha_{n}-1)M_{\mu}+(\beta_{m}-1)M_{\nu}\Bigr).\end{aligned}

(59)

By Lemma
9
, we also obtain

\displaystyle s_{n,m}\leq e\sqrt{M_{\mu}M_{\nu}}.

(60)

We then obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{UOT}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m})-\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})\\
&\leq\Bigl(M_{\mu}|\alpha_{n}-1|+M_{\nu}|\beta_{m}-1|+e\sqrt{M_{\mu}M_{\nu}}\bigl(|\log\alpha_{n}|+|\log\beta_{m}|\bigr)\Bigr).\end{aligned}

(61)

Next, Proposition
7
applies to
(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})
because
\widetilde{\mu}_{n}(\Omega)=M_{\mu}
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{m}(\Omega)=M_{\nu}
. Hence

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})-\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)\\
&\leq\int\zeta_{0}\mathrm{d}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n}-\mu)+\int\xi_{0}\mathrm{d}(\widetilde{\nu}_{m}-\nu)+C_{\Lambda}\Bigl(M_{\mu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\mu}_{n},\bar{\mu})+M_{\nu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\nu}_{m},\bar{\nu})\Bigr).\end{aligned}

(62)

Adding (
61
) and (
62
) proves (
56
).

We now derive (
57
).
Taking expectations in (
56
), the linear terms

\mathbb{E}[\int\zeta_{0}d(\widetilde{\mu}_{n}-\mu)]
and
\mathbb{E}[\int\xi_{0}d(\widetilde{\nu}_{m}-\nu)]

vanish by unbiasedness of
\widetilde{\mu}_{n}=M_{\mu}\bar{\mu}_{n}
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{m}=M_{\nu}\bar{\nu}_{m}
.
By the a.s. lower bound
\widehat{M}_{\mu}\geq cM_{\mu}
in Assumption
4
,
\alpha_{n}\geq c
a.s., so the mean-value theorem applied to
\log
on
[c,\infty)
gives

|\log\alpha_{n}|\leq|\alpha_{n}-1|/c

a.s., and analogously
|\log\beta_{m}|\leq|\beta_{m}-1|/c
a.s. Hence, using the identities
M_{\mu}|\alpha_{n}-1|=|\widehat{M}_{\mu}-M_{\mu}|
and
M_{\nu}|\beta_{m}-1|=|\widehat{M}_{\nu}-M_{\nu}|
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[M_{\mu}|\alpha_{n}-1|+e\sqrt{M_{\mu}M_{\nu}}|\log\alpha_{n}|\bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[|\widehat{M}_{\mu}-M_{\mu}|]+\frac{e\sqrt{M_{\mu}M_{\nu}}}{cM_{\mu}}\mathbb{E}[|\widehat{M}_{\mu}-M_{\mu}|]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+c^{-1}e\sqrt{M_{\nu}/M_{\mu}}\right)a_{n},

and analogously
\mathbb{E}\bigl[M_{\nu}|\beta_{m}-1|+e\sqrt{M_{\mu}M_{\nu}}|\log\beta_{m}|\bigr]\leq\left(1+c^{-1}e\sqrt{M_{\mu}/M_{\nu}}\right)b_{m}
. Setting

C_{M_{\mu},M_{\nu}}\coloneqq 1+c^{-1}e\sqrt{M_{\mu}/M_{\nu}}+c^{-1}e\sqrt{M_{\nu}/M_{\mu}}

and absorbing the constant yields (
57
).
The final convergence claim is immediate from (
56
) together with
\alpha_{n},\beta_{m}\to 1
and
|\log\alpha_{n}|,|\log\beta_{m}|\to 0
a.s.
∎

D.3.
Supportive Results

In preparation, we introduce the following result to characterize the Monge map.

Proposition 11

(Monge structure of
T_{0}
)
.

Assume that the population UOT optimizer is deterministic, namely

\displaystyle\gamma=(\mathrm{id},T_{0})_{\#}\gamma_{0}.

(63)

Then
\gamma
is an optimal coupling for the
balanced
Kantorovich problem between the active marginals
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{2}}c(x,y)d\gamma(x,y)=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})}\int_{\Omega^{2}}c(x,y)d\pi(x,y).

(64)

Consequently,
T_{0}
solves the Monge problem

\displaystyle\inf_{T:T_{\#}\gamma_{0}=\gamma_{1}}\int_{\Omega}c(x,T(x))d\gamma_{0}(x).

(65)

If, in addition,
c(x,y)=\tfrac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}
and
\gamma_{0}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure, then this Monge solution is unique
\gamma_{0}
-almost everywhere.

Proof of Proposition
11
.

Fix any
\pi\in\Pi(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})
. Since
\pi
has the same marginals as
\gamma
, the
D_{\mathrm{KL}}
terms in the UOT objective coincide:

\displaystyle{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\pi_{0}\mid\mu)={D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\gamma_{0}\mid\mu),\qquad{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\pi_{1}\mid\nu)={D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\gamma_{1}\mid\nu).

(66)

Because
\gamma
minimizes the UOT objective, we therefore obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{2}}c(x,y)d\gamma(x,y)\leq\int_{\Omega^{2}}c(x,y)d\pi(x,y)\qquad\forall\pi\in\Pi(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}),

(67)

which proves the balanced Kantorovich optimality. Since
\gamma=(\mathrm{id},T_{0})_{\#}\gamma_{0}
, we have
T_{0\#}\gamma_{0}=\gamma_{1}
and

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{2}}c(x,y)d\gamma(x,y)=\int_{\Omega}c(x,T_{0}(x))d\gamma_{0}(x),

(68)

so
T_{0}
attains the infimum in (
65
).
For uniqueness, let
S:\Omega\to\Omega
be any other minimizer in (
65
).
Then
(\mathrm{id},S)_{\#}\gamma_{0}
is also an optimal coupling in
\Pi(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})
.
Because
\gamma_{0}
is absolutely continuous and the cost is
\tfrac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}
, the balanced transport problem between
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
has a unique optimal plan, and that plan is induced by a map.
Hence

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id},S)_{\#}\gamma_{0}=\gamma=(\mathrm{id},T_{0})_{\#}\gamma_{0},

which implies
S=T_{0}

\gamma_{0}
-almost everywhere.
∎

We will need the following two lemmas in order to prove the stability bound:

Lemma 12

(Lipschitzness of
\nabla\zeta_{0}
and
\nabla\xi_{0}
)
.

Assume Assumptions
1
and
2
, and define

\displaystyle\zeta_{0}(x)\coloneqq-\bigl(e^{-\varphi_{0}(x)}-1\bigr),\qquad\xi_{0}(y)\coloneqq-\bigl(e^{-\psi_{0}(y)}-1\bigr).

Then
\nabla\zeta_{0}
and
\nabla\xi_{0}
are Lipschitz on
\Omega
.

Proof of Lemma
12
.

We first prove this for
\zeta_{0}
. The gradient is
\nabla\zeta_{0}(x)=e^{-\varphi_{0}(x)}\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)
. By the product rule,

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\zeta_{0}(x)=-e^{-\varphi_{0}(x)}(\nabla\varphi_{0}(x))(\nabla\varphi_{0}(x))^{\top}+e^{-\varphi_{0}(x)}\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0}(x).

By Assumption
1
and
2
,
\varphi_{0}
and
\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)
are bounded on the compact set
\Omega
, and
\|\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0}(x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\Lambda\coloneqq\max(1-\kappa,\kappa^{-1}-1)
. Thus
\nabla\zeta_{0}
is
\Lambda
-Lipschitz.

For
\xi_{0}
, we have the dual relation
(I-\nabla^{2}\psi_{0})=(I-\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0})^{-1}

\mu
-a.e., which implies
(1-\kappa^{-1})I\preceq\nabla^{2}\psi_{0}\preceq(1-\kappa)I
. The rest of the proof remains identical to that of
\zeta_{0}
.
∎

Lemma 13

(Bound on
\int cd\widehat{\gamma}-\int cd\gamma
)
.

Let
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega\times\Omega)
have marginals
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
, and let
T_{\mu},T_{\nu}:\Omega\to\Omega
be measurable maps.
Define the pushforward coupling
\widehat{\gamma}=(T_{\mu},T_{\nu})_{\#}\gamma
. Then

\displaystyle\int cd\widehat{\gamma}-\int cd\gamma

\displaystyle\leq\int\|T_{\mu}(x)-x\|^{2}d\gamma_{0}(x)+\int\|T_{\nu}(y)-y\|^{2}d\gamma_{1}(y)

(69)

\displaystyle+\int\langle T_{\mu}(x)-x,x-y\rangle d\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle+\int\langle T_{\nu}(y)-y,y-x\rangle d\gamma(x,y).

Proof of Lemma
13
.

By definition of the pushforward,

\displaystyle\int c(x,y)d\widehat{\gamma}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\int\|T_{\mu}(x)-T_{\nu}(y)\|^{2}d\gamma(x,y).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\|T_{\mu}(x)-T_{\nu}(y)\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\|(T_{\mu}(x)-x)-(T_{\nu}(y)-y)+(x-y)\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|T_{\mu}(x)-x\|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|T_{\nu}(y)-y\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\langle x-y,T_{\mu}(x)-x\rangle-\langle x-y,T_{\nu}(y)-y\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\langle T_{\mu}(x)-x,T_{\nu}(y)-y\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}+\|T_{\mu}(x)-x\|^{2}+\|T_{\nu}(y)-y\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\langle T_{\mu}(x)-x,x-y\rangle+\langle T_{\nu}(y)-y,y-x\rangle.

Integrating this inequality with respect to
d\gamma(x,y)
and using the identities

\displaystyle\int\|T_{\mu}(x)-x\|^{2}d\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int\|T_{\mu}(x)-x\|^{2}d\gamma_{0}(x),

\displaystyle\int\|T_{\nu}(y)-y\|^{2}d\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int\|T_{\nu}(y)-y\|^{2}d\gamma_{1}(y),

yields (
69
).
∎

Appendix E
Proof of Theorem
3

We now formulate a first empirical two-sample analogue of
[
24
, Proposition 13]
for the
D_{\mathrm{KL}}
-penalized unbalanced problem.
The main point is that, in the unbalanced case, the natural excess quantity is no longer purely a transport mismatch term.
Besides the deviation of
Y_{j}
from the population Monge map values
T_{0}(X_{i})
from Proposition
11
, one must also keep track of how the fitted row and column masses differ from the
active
population marginals transported by the optimal Monge pair
(T_{0},\lambda_{0})
.

Let
\widetilde{\gamma}=(\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij})
be any optimizer of
\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})
, and write

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{i}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij},\qquad\widetilde{s}_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij}.

(70)

We also denote the atomic masses of the oracle empirical measures by

\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\coloneqq{M_{\mu}}/{n}
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{j}\coloneqq{M_{\nu}}/{m}
.
Finally, recall that for the population optimizer
(\varphi_{0},\psi_{0})
we have

\gamma_{0}=e^{-\varphi_{0}}\mu
and
\gamma_{1}=e^{-\psi_{0}}\nu
,
and
\zeta_{0}(x)=-(e^{-\varphi_{0}(x)}-1)
,
\xi_{0}(y)=-(e^{-\psi_{0}(y)}-1)
. Throughout the proof, we denote
c(x,y)=\tfrac{1}{2}\|x-y\|^{2}
.

Proposition 14

(Empirical UOT excess identity)
.

Define the oracle empirical active marginals

r_{i}^{\star}\coloneqq e^{-\varphi_{0}(X_{i})}\widetilde{\mu}_{i}
and
s_{j}^{\star}\coloneqq e^{-\psi_{0}(Y_{j})}\widetilde{\nu}_{j}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})-\int\zeta_{0}d\widetilde{\mu}_{n}-\int\xi_{0}d\widetilde{\nu}_{m}

(71)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij}\Bigl(c(X_{i},Y_{j})-\varphi_{0}(X_{i})-\psi_{0}(Y_{j})\Bigr)+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widetilde{r}\mid r^{\star})+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widetilde{s}\mid s^{\star}).

In particular, the right-hand side is nonnegative.
Moreover, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij}\Bigl(c(X_{i},Y_{j})-\varphi_{0}(X_{i})-\psi_{0}(Y_{j})\Bigr)+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widetilde{r}\mid r^{\star})+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widetilde{s}\mid s^{\star})\Biggr]

(72)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})-\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)\Bigr].

Proof of Proposition
14
.

Since
\widetilde{\gamma}
is optimal for
\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}c(X_{i},Y_{j})\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij}+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widetilde{r}\mid\widetilde{\mu})+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widetilde{s}\mid\widetilde{\nu}).

(73)

By the definition of
r_{i}^{\star}=e^{-\varphi_{0}(X_{i})}\widetilde{\mu}_{i}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widetilde{r}\mid\widetilde{\mu})&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\widetilde{r}_{i}\log\left(\frac{\widetilde{r}_{i}}{r_{i}^{\star}}\right)-\widetilde{r}_{i}+r_{i}^{\star}\right]-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{r}_{i}\log\left(\frac{r_{i}^{\star}}{\widetilde{\mu}_{i}}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\widetilde{\mu}_{i}-r_{i}^{\star})\\
&={D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widetilde{r}\mid r^{\star})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{r}_{i}\varphi_{0}(X_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{0}(X_{i})\widetilde{\mu}_{i},\end{aligned}

(74)

where the second equality follows

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{r_{i}^{\star}}{\widetilde{\mu}_{i}}\right)=-\varphi_{0}(X_{i}),\qquad\left(1-e^{-\varphi_{0}(X_{i})}\right)=\zeta_{0}(X_{i}).

(75)

Similarly, we obtain

{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widetilde{s}\mid\widetilde{\nu})={D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widetilde{s}\mid s^{\star})-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{s}_{j}\psi_{0}(Y_{j})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\xi_{0}(Y_{j})\widetilde{\nu}_{j}.

(76)

Substituting (
74
) and (
76
) into (
73
), and using the relations

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{r}_{i}\varphi_{0}(X_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij}\varphi_{0}(X_{i}),\qquad\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{s}_{j}\psi_{0}(Y_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij}\psi_{0}(Y_{j}),

(77)

we obtain the exact identity (
71
). Nonnegativity follows because the dual constraint gives

\displaystyle c(X_{i},Y_{j})-\varphi_{0}(X_{i})-\psi_{0}(Y_{j})\geq 0

(78)

for every
i,j
, and both discrete
D_{\mathrm{KL}}
terms are nonnegative.

Finally, taking expectations in (
71
) and using the i.i.d. sampling model together with the oracle masses, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int\zeta_{0}d\widetilde{\mu}_{n}\right]=\int\zeta_{0}d\mu,\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\int\xi_{0}d\widetilde{\nu}_{m}\right]=\int\xi_{0}d\nu.

(79)

Since we have the equality
\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)=\int\zeta_{0}d\mu+\int\xi_{0}d\nu
by the duality, we obtain (
72
).
∎

Corollary 15

(Bound on
\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}}]
)
.

Assume that Assumption
1
holds.
Let
\widetilde{\gamma}=(\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij})
be any optimizer of
\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})
, and define

\displaystyle\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij}\|T_{0}(X_{i})-Y_{j}\|^{2}.

(80)

Then, almost surely,

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{2}\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}}\leq\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})-\int\zeta_{0}d\widetilde{\mu}_{n}-\int\xi_{0}d\widetilde{\nu}_{m}.

(81)

Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}}]\leq\frac{2}{\kappa}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})-\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)\Bigr].

(82)

If, in addition, Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
hold, then with
C_{\Lambda}
from Proposition
7
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}}]\leq\frac{2C_{\Lambda}}{\kappa}\left(\mathbb{E}[M_{\mu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\mu}_{n},\bar{\mu})]+\mathbb{E}[M_{\nu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\nu}_{m},\bar{\nu})]\right).

(83)

Proof of Corollary
15
.

By Proposition
14
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})-\int\zeta_{0}d\widetilde{\mu}_{n}-\int\xi_{0}d\widetilde{\nu}_{m}\\
&\qquad=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij}\Bigl(c(X_{i},Y_{j})-\varphi_{0}(X_{i})-\psi_{0}(Y_{j})\Bigr)+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widetilde{r}\mid r^{\star})+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widetilde{s}\mid s^{\star}).\end{aligned}

(84)

Lemma
2
and the nonnegativity of the discrete
D_{\mathrm{KL}}
terms imply

\displaystyle\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})-\int\zeta_{0}d\widetilde{\mu}_{n}-\int\xi_{0}d\widetilde{\nu}_{m}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij}\frac{\kappa}{2}\|Y_{j}-T_{0}(X_{i})\|^{2}

(85)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{2}\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}},

(86)

which proves (
81
). Taking expectations and using (
72
) yields (
82
).

Finally, Proposition
7
applied to
(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})
gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})-\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)

(87)

\displaystyle\leq\int\zeta_{0}d(\widetilde{\mu}_{n}-\mu)+\int\xi_{0}d(\widetilde{\nu}_{m}-\nu)+C_{\Lambda}\Bigl(M_{\mu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\mu}_{n},\bar{\mu})+M_{\nu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\nu}_{m},\bar{\nu})\Bigr).

(88)

Taking expectations, the linear terms vanish by unbiasedness of the oracle empirical measures, and (
83
) follows.
∎

Remark 2
.

Corollary
15
is the closest UOT analogue of the balanced case in
[
24
, Proposition 13]
.
The important difference from the balanced case is that the empirical excess objective contains, in addition to the transport mismatch
\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}}
, two positive
D_{\mathrm{KL}}
terms encoding estimation error of the active source and target masses.
Because of these extra terms, the argument above yields a clean
one-sided
control of
\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}}]
by the excess UOT objective, but not a reverse inequality without further information on the fitted empirical marginals.

E.1.
From
\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}}
to barycentric and 1NN map errors

We now show how Corollary
15
feeds directly into concrete estimators of the transport map.
The first step is to pass from the row sums of
\widetilde{\gamma}
to the barycentric projection
\widetilde{T}_{i}
.
The second step is to observe that the one-nearest-neighbor extension inherits the same in-sample error on the empirical active source measure.

Theorem 16

(Oracle barycentric and 1NN bounds)
.

Assume that Assumption
1
holds.
For each
i
with
\widetilde{r}_{i}>0
, define the barycentric projection of the
i
th row of
\widetilde{\gamma}
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{i}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\widetilde{r}_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij}Y_{j},

(89)

and set
\widetilde{T}_{i}=X_{i}
when
\widetilde{r}_{i}=0
.
Define

\displaystyle\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{r}_{i}\|\widetilde{T}_{i}-T_{0}(X_{i})\|^{2}.

(90)

Then, almost surely,

\displaystyle\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}\leq\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}}.

(91)

Consequently, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}\bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\kappa}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathrm{UOT}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\widetilde{\nu}_{m})-\mathrm{UOT}(\mu,\nu)\Bigr],

(92)

and, if Assumptions
1
-
3
hold, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}\bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2C_{\Lambda}}{\kappa}\left(\mathbb{E}[M_{\mu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\mu}_{n},\bar{\mu})]+\mathbb{E}[M_{\nu}W_{2}^{2}(\bar{\nu}_{m},\bar{\nu})]\right).

(93)

Now let
\widetilde{T}^{\mathrm{1NN}}
be the Voronoi extension defined above, and let

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{r}_{i}\delta_{X_{i}}

(94)

be the fitted empirical active source measure.
Then

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\|\widetilde{T}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(x)-T_{0,n}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(x)\|^{2}d\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}(x)=\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}.

(95)

Hence the same expectation bounds (
92
)-(
93
) hold for the in-sample error of the 1NN extension measured against
T_{0,n}^{\mathrm{1NN}}
on
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}
.

Proof of Theorem
16
.

Fix
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
If
\widetilde{r}_{i}=0
, then the
i
th contribution to
\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}
is zero and there is nothing to prove.
Assume therefore that
\widetilde{r}_{i}>0
.
By the barycentric formula for the
i
th row of
\widetilde{\gamma}
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{i}-T_{0}(X_{i})=\frac{1}{\widetilde{r}_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij}\bigl(Y_{j}-T_{0}(X_{i})\bigr).

(96)

Since
z\mapsto\|z\|^{2}
is convex, Jensen’s inequality gives

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{T}_{i}-T_{0}(X_{i})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\widetilde{r}_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij}\|Y_{j}-T_{0}(X_{i})\|^{2}.

(97)

Multiplying by
\widetilde{r}_{i}
and summing over
i
yields

\displaystyle\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{r}_{i}\|\widetilde{T}_{i}-T_{0}(X_{i})\|^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{\gamma}_{ij}\|Y_{j}-T_{0}(X_{i})\|^{2}=\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}},

(98)

which proves (
91
).
The expectation bounds (
92
) and (
93
) now follow immediately from Corollary
15
.

For the 1NN claim, note that the support of
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}
is contained in
\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
and, for each
i
, one has

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(X_{i})=\widetilde{T}_{i},\qquad T_{0,n}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(X_{i})=T_{0}(X_{i}).

(99)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\|\widetilde{T}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(x)-T_{0,n}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(x)\|^{2}d\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{r}_{i}\|\widetilde{T}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(X_{i})-T_{0,n}^{\mathrm{1NN}}(X_{i})\|^{2}

(100)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{r}_{i}\|\widetilde{T}_{i}-T_{0}(X_{i})\|^{2}=\Delta_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}},

(101)

which is (
95
).
The last sentence follows by substituting this identity into (
92
)-(
93
).
∎

Theorem
16
is deliberately stated for the oracle empirical plan
\widetilde{\gamma}
so that it matches Corollary
15
without any additional bookkeeping.
For the actual fitted empirical plan based on
(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m})
, one obtains the following estimated-mass variant.

Proposition 17

(Bounds on
\widehat{\Delta}_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}}
and
\widehat{\Delta}_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}
)
.

Assume that Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
, and
4
hold.
Let
C_{\Lambda}
be the stability constant from Proposition
7
.
Define the empirical active measures

\displaystyle\widehat{r}_{i}^{\star}\coloneqq e^{-\varphi_{0}(X_{i})}\widehat{\mu}_{i},\qquad\widehat{s}_{j}^{\star}\coloneqq e^{-\psi_{0}(Y_{j})}\widehat{\nu}_{j},

(102)

and the fitted empirical transport and barycentric errors

\displaystyle\widehat{\Delta}_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{\gamma}_{ij}\|T_{0}(X_{i})-Y_{j}\|^{2},

(103)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Delta}_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{r}_{i}\|\widehat{T}_{i}-T_{0}(X_{i})\|^{2}.

(104)

Then, almost surely,

\displaystyle\mathrm{UOT}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m})-\int\zeta_{0}d\widehat{\mu}_{n}-\int\xi_{0}d\widehat{\nu}_{m}

(105)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widehat{\gamma}_{ij}\Bigl(c(X_{i},Y_{j})-\varphi_{0}(X_{i})-\psi_{0}(Y_{j})\Bigr)+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widehat{r}\mid\widehat{r}^{\star})+{D_{\mathrm{KL}}}(\widehat{s}\mid\widehat{s}^{\star}).

In particular,

\displaystyle\widehat{\Delta}_{nm}^{\mathrm{bar}}\leq\widehat{\Delta}_{nm}^{\mathrm{tr}}\leq\frac{2}{\kappa}\left[\mathrm{UOT}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\widehat{\nu}_{m})-\int\zeta_{0}d\widehat{\mu}_{n}-\int\xi_{0}d\widehat{\nu}_{m}\right].

(106)

Now let
